On the quantitative lower bounds for solutions to the Boltzmann equation in nonconvex domains
Jhe-kuan SU

Abstract
. In this article, we study the continuous mild solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a bounded spatial domain, under either angular cutoff assumption or non-cutoff assumption. Without assuming convexity of the spatial domain, we establish a Maxwellian lower bound in the cutoff case, and a weaker-than-Maxwellian lower bound for the non-cutoff case. This extends the results of [ 1 , 2 ] , where the convexity of the domain was required.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we investigate the lower bounds of the mild solutions to the Boltzmann equation on a spatial domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying

Assumption A
.

\Omega
is a connected, bounded open set with
C^{2}
boundary.

The Boltzmann equation under consideration reads

(1.1)

\partial_{t}f(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla f(t,x,v)=Q[f,f](t,x,v),\ \forall(t,x,v)\in[0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3},

(1.2)

f(0,x,v)=f_{0}(x,v),\ \forall(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3},

where
T>0
.
We consider the mixed boundary condition: for any
(x,v)\in\Gamma^{-}
, we have

(1.3)

f(x,v)=\alpha f(t,x,R(x,v))+(1-\alpha)\left(\int_{w\cdot n(x)>0}f(t,x,w)(w\cdot n(x))dw\right)\frac{1}{2\pi T_{B}^{2}}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2T_{B}}}

for some
T_{B}>0
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
. Here,
\Gamma^{-}:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}|n(x)\cdot v<0\}
,
R(x,v):=v-2(v\cdot n(x))n(x)
, where
n(x)
is the outward unit normal vector at the boundary point
x\in\partial\Omega
.

The collision operator
Q
is defined as

(1.4)

Q[h_{1},h_{2}](v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})[h_{2}(v^{\prime})h_{1}(v^{\prime}_{*})-h_{2}(v)h_{1}(v_{*})]\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma,

where
v^{\prime}
,
v_{*}
,
v^{\prime}_{*}
are defined as:

v^{\prime}:=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\ v^{\prime}_{*}:=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}-\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma

with
\cos{\theta}:=\left\langle\frac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|},\sigma\right\rangle
.
We assume that the collision kernel
B\geq 0
and satisfies:

Assumption B
.

(1.5)

B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})=\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)b(\cos{\theta}),

where
\Phi:=\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)
is a function defined on
[0,\infty)
satisfying

(1.6)

c_{\Phi}r^{\gamma}\leq\Phi(r)\leq C_{\Phi}r^{\gamma},\ \forall r\in[0,\infty),

or

(1.7)

\begin{cases}c_{\Phi}r^{\gamma}\leq\Phi(r)\leq C_{\Phi}r^{\gamma},\ &\forall r\in[1,\infty),\\
c_{\Phi}\leq\Phi(r)\leq C_{\Phi},\ &\forall r\in[0,1]\end{cases}

for some positive constants
c_{\Phi}
,
C_{\Phi}
and
\gamma\in(-3,1]
. Here, we assume that the function
b:\theta\mapsto b(\cos{\theta})
is continuous on

(0,\pi]
, positive near
\frac{\pi}{2}
, and satisfies

(1.8)

\lim\limits_{\theta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{b(\cos{\theta})\sin{\theta}}{\theta^{-(1+\nu)}}=b_{0},

for some
b_{0}>0
and
\nu\in(-\infty,2)
.

The problems of quantifying the positivity of the solutions to (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) have attracted considerable attention from many authors. Beyond interest in physics, the problem also plays a crucial role in kinetic theory. For example, the exponential lower bound is crucial in the study of the behavior of the solution to the related Laudau equation in
[
4
,
5
]
. In
[
6
]
, the Maxwellian lower bound is applied to derive the uniqueness of the solution to the Boltzmann equation in
L_{v}^{1}L_{x}^{\infty}(1+|v|^{2+0})
.
In 1933, Carleman
[
3
]
showed the existence of exponential lower bounds on the radially symmetric solution of the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation with angular cutoff hard potentials. Since then, many results have been derived. For example, in
[
12
]
Pulvirenti and Wennberg improved the result by proving the Maxwellian lower bound for non-radially symmetric solutions of the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation with angular cutoff hard potentials. In
[
11
]
, Mouhot removed the homogeneous assumption and obtained the Maxwellian lower bound for the torus domain.
This result was extended to the bounded convex domain with
C^{2}
boundary by Briant in
[
1
,
2
]
. We note that in the articles
[
1
,
2
,
11
]
, they also derived the ”less than Maxwellian” lower bounds for the non-cutoff case. Later, this result was improved to Maxwellian lower bounds in the case of hard and moderately soft potentials with the assumption that
\Omega
is a torus by Imbert, Mouhot, Silvestre in
[
9
]
and in the case where
\Omega
is
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by Henderson, Snelson, and Tarfulea in
[
8
]
.

However, the problem of Maxwellian lower bounds on a non-convex spatial domain remains an open problem in both the cutoff and non-cutoff cases. In this article, we derive a Maxwellian lower bound on an open bounded connected but not necessarily convex domain with a
C^{2}
boundary for the cutoff case. For the non-cutoff case, we also derive a weaker than Maxwellian lower bound.

In
[
1
,
2
,
11
]
, the following spreading property of the collision operator

(1.9)

Q^{+}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\delta)},\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\delta)}\right](v)\geq C\xi^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\delta\sqrt{2}(1-\xi))}(v),

for some
C>0
depending on the cross section, and any
\xi\in(0,1)
,
is a key ingredient to deriving a lower bound around one point, which could then be extended to any other point using the convexity of the domain.

In this article, we focus on the geometry of a
C^{2}
connected domain and show that for any two points of
\Omega
there always exists a suitable zigzag between these two points, which allows the spreading of the lower bound from one point to the other point like in
[
1
,
2
]
. One major difficulty arises from the requirement that these zigzags in the domain must remain sufficiently distant from the boundary of
\Omega
. In fact, the shape structure affects the amount of gas molecules passing through the boundary. In
[
1
,
2
]
, the lower bounds depend on: (1) hydrodynamic quantities. (2) the modulus of continuity of
f_{0}
. In our result, the lower bounds also depend on
\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(\Omega)
, the maximum number of ”good” zigzag segments needed to connect any two points in the spatial domain. We put the definition of
\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(\Omega)
in (
2.37
) and address the detailed analysis concerning the geometry in Section 2. Our main contribution lies in showing that the Maxwellian lower bound can be propagated throughout a non-convex domain by exploiting geometric connectivity properties, quantified through
\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(\Omega)
.

Throughout Sections 1–5, we assume that
\nu<0
(cutoff case), where
\nu
is defined in (
1.8
). To classify the type of solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.3
), we first decompose the collision operator
Q
as follows:

(1.10)

\begin{split}&Q[h_{1},h_{2}](v)\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})[h_{2}(v^{\prime})h_{1}(v^{\prime}_{*})-h_{2}(v)h_{1}(v_{*})]\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})[h_{2}(v^{\prime})h_{1}(v^{\prime}_{*})]\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma\\
&-h_{2}(v)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})h_{1}(v_{*})\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma\\
=&:Q^{+}[h_{1},h_{2}](v)-h_{2}(v)L[h_{1}](v),\end{split}

where

\displaystyle Q^{+}[h_{1},h_{2}](v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})[h_{2}(v^{\prime})h_{1}(v^{\prime}_{*})]\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma,

\displaystyle L[h_{1}](v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos{\theta})h_{1}(v_{*})\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma.

Next, we define the following notations:

\displaystyle t_{b}(x,v):=\sup\left\{\{0\}\cup\{t>0\mid x-sv\in\Omega,\,\forall\,0<s<t\}\right\},

\displaystyle x_{b}(x,v):=x-t_{b}(x,v)v,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid n(x)\cdot v=0\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid n(x)\cdot v>0\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{-}:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid n(x)\cdot v<0\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{-}^{0}:=\{(x,v)\in\Gamma^{0}\mid t_{b}(x,v)=0,t_{b}(x,-v)>0,\,\exists\,\delta>0\ni\,x-s^{\prime}v\in(\overline{\Omega})^{c}\,\forall\,s^{\prime}\in(0,\delta)\},

(1.11)

\begin{split}&\Gamma_{conti}:=\left\{\{0\}\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\right\}\cup\left\{(0,\infty)\times(\Gamma^{-}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{-})\right\}\\
&\cup\left\{(t,x,v)\in(0,T)\times\{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\cup\Gamma^{+}\}\ |t<t_{b}(x,v)\,\mathrm{or}\,(x_{b}(x,v),v)\in\Gamma^{-}\cup\Gamma^{0}_{-})\right\}.\end{split}

Using the above notations and decomposition, we are ready to introduce the continuous mild solutions of the Boltzmann equation:

Definition 1.1
.

We assume that the domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
Assumption A
and the collision kernel
B
satisfies
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
, where
\nu
is introduced in (
1.8
). Given a nonnegative, continuous function
f_{0}
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we call a nonnegative function
f
defined on
[0,T)\times(\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
|f(t,x,v)|\leq C(1+|v|)^{-r}
for some constant
C>0
and
r>3
for any
0<t\leq T
,
(x,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
a ”continuous mild” solution to (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) with initial data
f_{0}
if
f
is continuous on
\Gamma_{conti}
and for
(t,x,v)\in[0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the function
f(t,x,v)
satisfies the following integral identities:

(1.12)

\begin{split}f(t,x,v)&=f_{0}(X_{0,t}(x,v),v)\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}L[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds\right)\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{t}L[f(s^{\prime},X_{s^{\prime},t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds^{\prime}\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274ptQ^{+}[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot),f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds,\end{split}

when
t\leq t_{\partial}(x,v):=\sup\{t\geq 0|x-vs\in\overline{\Omega},\ \forall s\in[0,t]\}
, and

(1.13)

\begin{split}f(t,x,v)&=\alpha f(t-t_{\partial}(x,v),X_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v),t}(x,v),R(X_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v),t}(x,v),v))\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\exp\left(-\int_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v)}^{t}L[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds\right)\\
&+(1-\alpha)\left(\int_{w\cdot n(X_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v),t}(x,v))>0}f(t,X_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v),t}(x,v),w)(w\cdot n(X_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v),t}(x,v)))dw\right)\\
&\hskip 51.21504pt\frac{1}{2\pi T_{B}^{2}}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2T_{B}}}\exp\left(-\int_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v)}^{t}L[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds\right)\\
&+\int_{t-t_{\partial}(x,v)}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{t}L[f(s^{\prime},X_{s^{\prime},t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds^{\prime}\right)\\
&\hskip 39.83368ptQ^{+}[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot),f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds,\end{split}

when
t\geq t_{\partial}(x,v)
.
Here, a detailed definition of the characteristic line
X_{s,t}(x,v)
is provided in the
Appendix
.

Remark 1.1
.

In the case of specular reflection (
\alpha=1
), the continuous mild solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) can also be written as the continuous function on
\Gamma_{conti}
,
f
such that for all
(t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

(1.14)

\begin{split}f(t,x,v)&=f_{0}(X_{0,t}(x,v),V_{0,t}(x,v))\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}L[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](V_{s,t}(x,v))\,ds\right)\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{t}L[f(s^{\prime},X_{s^{\prime},t}(x,v),\cdot)](V_{s^{\prime},t}(x,v))\,ds^{\prime}\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274ptQ^{+}[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot),f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](V_{s,t}(x,v))\,ds,\end{split}

where
V_{s,t}
is defined in the
Appendix
.

Remark 1.2
.

The continuity assumption of the solution
f(t,x,v)
is technical, as the proof only involves the continuity of the initial condition.
Nonetheless, in the diffusive reflection boundary condition (
\alpha=0
), the existence of a solution of bounded variation is established in
[
7
]
. However, as shown in
[
10
]
, unlike in the convex case, the solution loses continuity, with singularities propagating along the grazing set. Motivated by
[
10
]
, we therefore consider the set
\Gamma_{conti}
. In the same work, the authors also showed that the continuity of solutions on
\Gamma_{conti}
is ensured under diffuse reflection boundary conditions, with continuity of the initial data and addition conditions.

Before stating our main result, we introduce the following hydrodynamic quantities:

(1.15)

\varrho_{f}(t.x):=\int_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}f^{2}(t,x,v)\,dv

(1.16)

e_{f}(t,x):=\int_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v|^{2}f(t,x,v)\,dv,\,E_{f}:=\sup\limits_{[0,T)\times\Omega}(e_{f}(t,x)+\mathcal{\varrho}_{f}(t.x)),

(1.17)

l_{f,p}(t,x):=\left(\int_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}f^{p}(t,x,v)\,dv\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\,L_{f,p}:=\sup\limits_{[0,T)\times\Omega}l_{f,p}(t,x),

It is also known that the quantity (total mass)

(1.18)

M:=\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\,dx\,dv

does not depend on
t
, where
f
is a (continuous mild) solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
).

We can now state our main result as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
Assumption A
and that the kernel
B
satisfies
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
. We consider a non-negative continuous function
f_{0}
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
f(t,x,v)
be a continuous mild solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) on
[0,T)\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with initial condition
f_{0}
for some
T>0
, and let
\alpha\in[0,1]
. We assume that
f(t,x,v)
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

M>0
;

(2)

E_{f}<\infty
if
\gamma\geq 0
and
\max\{E_{f},L_{f,{p_{\gamma}}}\}<\infty
, where
p_{\gamma}>\frac{3}{3+\gamma}>0
, if
-3<\gamma<0
.

Then, the following lower bound holds:
There exists
0<\tau_{0}\leq T
such that for any
\tau\in(0,\tau_{0})
, there exist
\Delta_{\tau_{0}}>0
,
\rho>0
and
\theta>0
depending on
\tau_{0}
,
M
,
C_{\Phi},c_{\Phi},\gamma,b_{0},\nu,E_{f}
(and
L_{f,p}
if
\gamma<0
),
\tau
,
\alpha
,
\Omega
and on the modulus of continuity of
f_{0}
, such that

(1.19)

f(t,x,v)\geq\frac{\rho}{(2\pi\theta)^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2\theta}},\ \forall\,t\in[\tau,\Delta_{\tau_{0}}),\ \forall\,(x,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

The next theorem can be derived from
Theorem
1.1
:

Theorem 1.2
.

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
Assumption A
and that the kernel
B
satisfies
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
. We consider a non-negative continuous function
f_{0}
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
f(t,x,v)
be a continuous mild solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) on
[0,T)\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with initial condition
f_{0}
for some
T>0
, and let
\alpha\in[0,1]
. We assume that
f(t,x,v)
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

M>0
;

(2)

E_{f}<\infty
if
\gamma\geq 0
and
\max\{E_{f},L_{f,{p_{\gamma}}}\}<\infty
, where
p_{\gamma}>\frac{3}{3+\gamma}>0
, if
-3<\gamma<0
.

Then, the following lower bound holds:
For any
\tau\in(0,T)
, there exist
\rho>0
and
\theta>0
depending on
M
,
C_{\Phi},c_{\Phi},\gamma,b_{0},\nu,E_{f}
(and
L_{f,p}
if
\gamma<0
),
\tau
,
\alpha
,
\Omega
and on the modulus of continuity of
f_{0}
, such that

(1.20)

f(t,x,v)\geq\frac{\rho}{(2\pi\theta)^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2\theta}},\ \forall\,t\in[\tau,T),\ \forall\,(x,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

For the non-cutoff case, the result is stated in Chapter 6. We note that the numbers
\rho
and
\theta
are computable thanks to the special zigzag which will be introduced in
Proposition
2.5
, through which we can guarantee that a certain amount and range of the lower bound can be generated through the propagation in
Proposition
3.4
and derive a computable uniform lower bound (which depends on
\Omega
since the construction of the zigzag is affected by the geometric properties of
\Omega
) as in
Proposition
3.5
.

The remainder of the article is organized as follows. In Section 2, we analyze the boundary of non-convex domain to derive some key properties. In Section 3, we focus on the derivation of a series of diluting initial lower bounds and the propagation effect of lower bounds. In Section 4 and Section 5, we provide a detailed proof of the Maxwellian lower bound on mild solution to the Boltzmann equation with non-fully specular (
0\leq\alpha<1
) and fully specular reflection boundary condition (
\alpha=1
), respectively. In Section 6, a weaker exponential lower bound of solutions to the Boltzmann equation for non-cutoff case is provided.

2.
Geometric properties near the boundary

In this section, we introduce some useful lemmas.

Lemma 2.1
.

Given
\Omega
which satisfies
Assumption A
, there exists
\delta:=\delta(\Omega)>0
, such that for any
0<d<\min\{1,\delta\}
, one can find
m_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{x^{0}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\in\partial\Omega
depending on
d
such that

(2.1)

\partial\Omega\subset\underset{1\leq i\leq m_{1}}{\bigcup}B\left(x_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{8}\right)

so that, for each
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
, after orthogonal transformation of variables, the boundary near
x_{i}^{0}
can be represented as a graph of a
C^{2}
scalar function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. More precisely, for each
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
, there exists a orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}^{1},e_{i}^{2},-n(x_{i}^{0})\}
and a
C^{2}
function
\phi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(0,0)=0,\nabla\phi_{i}(0,0)=0,

(2.2)

\begin{split}&\partial\Omega\cap B(x_{i}^{0},3d)=\{y\in B(x_{i}^{0},3d)\mid y=x_{i}^{0}+u_{1}e_{i}^{1}+u_{2}e_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}(u_{1},u_{2})n(x^{0}_{i})\},\\
&\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x_{i}^{0},3d)}=\{y\in\overline{B(x_{i}^{0},3d)}\mid y=x_{i}^{0}+u_{1}e_{i}^{1}+u_{2}e_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}(u_{1},u_{2})n(x^{0}_{i})\},\\
&\Omega\cap B(x_{i}^{0},3d)=\{y\in B(x_{i}^{0},3d)\mid\\
&\hskip 99.58464pty=x_{i}^{0}+u_{1}e_{i}^{1}+u_{2}e_{i}^{2}-u_{3}n(x^{0}_{i}),u_{3}>\phi_{i}(u_{1},u_{2})\}.\end{split}

Furthermore, we have
|\nabla\phi_{i}|<\frac{1}{100}
,
|\nabla^{2}\phi_{i}|<\widetilde{C}(\Omega)
for some
\widetilde{C}(\Omega)>\max\left\{\frac{4}{d},\frac{1}{2d_{r}}\right\}
, and for any
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
,

(2.3)

B\left(x_{i}^{0}-\frac{d}{2}n(x_{i}^{0}),\frac{d}{2}\right)\subset B\left(x_{i}^{0},d\right)\cap\Omega.

Here,
d_{r}
is introduced in
Remark
2.1
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the application of the Heine-Borel theorem.
∎

Remark 2.1
.

It is known that any bounded domain with
C^{2}
boundary satisfies the uniform interior sphere condition. That is, there exists
d_{r}
such that for any
x\in\partial\Omega
, one can find a ball with radius
d_{r}>0
in
\Omega
that intersects with
\partial\Omega
only on
x
. In this article, we choose
d
from
Lemma
2.1
to be smaller than
d_{r}
.

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy
Assumption A
with
\delta:=\delta(\Omega)>0
. For any
0<d<\min\{1,\delta\}
, which is given in
Lemma
2.1
, given
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
, if
x\in B(x^{0}_{i},\frac{3d}{2})
with coordinate representation

x=x_{i}^{0}+x_{1}e^{1}_{i}+x_{2}e^{2}_{i}-x_{3}n(x_{i}^{0}),

then

(2.4)

d(x,\partial\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{i},3d))\geq\frac{1}{2}(x_{3}-\phi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the fact that
|\nabla\phi_{i}|<\frac{1}{100}
and
the mean value theorem. Indeed, given
x=x_{i}^{0}+x_{1}e^{1}_{i}+x_{2}e^{2}_{i}-x_{3}n(x_{i}^{0})
, if there exists
x^{\prime}=x_{i}^{0}+x^{\prime}_{1}e^{1}_{i}+x^{\prime}_{2}e^{2}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{3}n(x_{i}^{0})\in B(x,\frac{1}{2}(x_{3}-\phi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})))
such that
x^{\prime}\in\partial\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{i},3d))
, we have by
Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})=x^{\prime}_{3}.

By the mean value theorem, there exists
\widetilde{x}_{1}
between
x_{1}
and
x^{\prime}_{1}
,
\widetilde{x}_{2}
between
x_{2}
and
x^{\prime}_{2}
such that

(2.5)

|\nabla\phi_{i}(\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2})|=\frac{|\phi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})-\phi_{i}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})|}{|(x_{1},x_{2})-(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})|}\geq\frac{\frac{1}{2}(x_{3}-\phi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}))}{\frac{1}{2}(x_{3}-\phi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}))}=1,

from which we deduce a contradiction to the fact that
|\nabla\phi_{i}(\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2})|<\frac{1}{100}
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Notice that by the triangle inequality we have
\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}\subset\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{m_{1}}B(x_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{4})
, where
\Omega_{\epsilon}:=\{x\in\Omega|\ d(x,\partial\Omega)<\epsilon\}
.

Next, we define
y_{i}^{0}:=x_{i}^{0}-\frac{3d}{4}n(x_{i}^{0})
. Observe that
y_{i}^{0}\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, since
B(y_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{8})\subset B\left(x_{i}^{0}-\frac{d}{2}n(x_{i}^{0}),\frac{d}{2}\right)\subset B\left(x_{i}^{0},d\right)\cap\Omega
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Given
\Omega
which satisfies
Assumption A
. Let
0<d<\min(1,\delta)
and
y_{i}^{0}\in\Omega
as defined in
Lemma
2.1
.

Then, there exist
m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{y_{i}^{0}\}_{i=m_{1}+1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
such that

(1)

For any
x\in\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, there exists
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
such that
\overline{xy_{i}^{0}}\subset\Omega
.

(2)

For any
x\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, there exists
m_{1}+1\leq i\leq m_{1}+m_{2}
with the property that
x\in B(y_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{16})
and
\overline{xy_{i}^{0}}\subset\Omega
.

Here,
\overline{xy}:=\{sx+(1-s)y\mid 0\leq s\leq 1\}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma
2.1
and
Remark
2.2
, there exist
m_{1}
and
\{x_{i}^{0}\}_{i=1}^{m_{1}}
such that

(2.6)

\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{m_{1}}B\left(x_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{4}\right).

Because of the representation of
\partial\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{i},d)
in
Lemma
2.1
, we see that

(2.7)

\overline{xy_{i}^{0}}\in\Omega,\ \forall x\in B\left(y_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{4}\right).

Next, we cover
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
by
\{B(x,\frac{d}{16})\}_{x\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}}
. Since
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
is compact. The Heine-Borel covering theorem ensures the existence of a finite sub-cover of this collection (Here, we choose the sub-cover with the minimum number of covers). We then denote the corresponding centers by
\{y_{i}^{0}\}_{i=m_{1}+1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}
. Finally, we conclude the proof by observing that

B\left(y_{i}^{0},\frac{d}{16}\right)\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{16}}\subset\Omega,\ \forall\,m_{1}+1\leq i\leq m_{1}+m_{2}.

∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
\Omega
which satisfies
Assumption A
, with
\delta
and
d
as given in
Lemma
2.1
. For any
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
, given

\displaystyle x=x^{0}_{i}+x_{1}e^{1}_{i}+x_{2}e^{2}_{i}-x_{3}n(x^{0}_{i}),

\displaystyle y=x^{0}_{i}+y_{1}e^{1}_{i}+y_{2}e^{2}_{i}-y_{3}n(x^{0}_{i})

with
x\in\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},\frac{d}{4})},\,y\in\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},\frac{3d}{4})}
,
(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{d}{4}
, and
\frac{x-y}{|x-y|}\cdot n(x^{0}_{i})\geq\frac{1}{2}
,

we have

(2.8)

d(\overline{xy},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)})=d(x,\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)}).

Proof.

Note that it suffices to show that for any
0\leq q_{1}<1
, there exists
\epsilon^{\prime}(q_{1})>0
such that for any
q_{1}<q_{2}<q_{1}+\epsilon^{\prime}(q_{1})
, we have
d(q_{1}x+(1-q_{1})y,\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)})>d(q_{2}x+(1-q_{2})y,\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)})
.

Figure 2.1.
A figure illustrating how the points in the proof of Lemma
2.3
is located.

To show that, we first notice that by non-emptiness and compactness of
\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)}
, there exists a point
z^{\prime\prime}\in\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)}
such that

\displaystyle|z^{\prime\prime}-q_{1}x-(1-q_{1})y|=d(q_{1}x+(1-q_{1})y,\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)}).

We parameterize
x
,
y
,
z^{\prime\prime}
as follows:

\displaystyle x=x^{0}_{i}+x_{1}e^{1}_{i}+x_{2}e^{2}_{i}-x_{3}n(x^{0}_{i}),

\displaystyle y=x^{0}_{i}+y_{1}e^{1}_{i}+y_{2}e^{2}_{i}-y_{3}n(x^{0}_{i}),

\displaystyle z^{\prime\prime}=x^{0}_{i}+z^{\prime\prime}_{1}e^{1}_{i}+z^{\prime\prime}_{2}e^{2}_{i}-z^{\prime\prime}_{3}n(x^{0}_{i}).

We define the following quantities:

(2.9)

\displaystyle Z_{1}:=\frac{(z^{\prime\prime}-q_{1}x-(1-q_{1})y)}{|z^{\prime\prime}-q_{1}x-(1-q_{1})y|}=\frac{z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}}}{|z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}}|},

(2.10)

\displaystyle Z_{2}:=\frac{x-q_{1}x-(1-q_{1})y}{|x-q_{1}x-(1-q_{1})y|}=\frac{x-y}{|x-y|}.

Before we proceed, we also define
z_{q_{1}}:=q_{1}x+(1-q_{1})y
, and we notice that

Z_{1}=n(z^{\prime\prime}).

By
Lemma
2.1
, we have (we recall
Z_{1}
from (
2.9
))

\displaystyle Z_{1}\cdot n(x^{0}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n(z^{\prime\prime})\cdot n(x^{0}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\partial_{1}\phi_{i}(z^{\prime\prime}_{1},z^{\prime\prime}_{2})|^{2}+|\partial_{2}\phi_{i}(z^{\prime\prime}_{1},z^{\prime\prime}_{2})|^{2}+1}}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{10000}{10001}}.

Notice that we have from the assumption of
Lemma
2.3
that (we recall
Z_{2}
from (
2.10
))

\displaystyle n(x^{0}_{i})\cdot Z_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Hence, we deduce that

(2.11)

\begin{split}&Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}\\
=&n(x^{0}_{i})\cdot Z_{2}+(Z_{1}-n(x^{0}_{i}))\cdot Z_{2}\\
\geq&\frac{1}{2}-|Z_{1}-n(x^{0}_{i})||Z_{2}|\\
=&\frac{1}{2}-\sqrt{|Z_{1}|^{2}+|n(x^{0}_{i})|^{2}-2Z_{1}\cdot n(x^{0}_{i})}\\
>&\frac{1}{2}-\sqrt{2\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{10000}{10001}}\right)}\\
>&\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}.\end{split}

Now, we define

\displaystyle W_{1,//}:=((z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}})\cdot Z_{2})Z_{2}=|z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}}|(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2})Z_{2},

\displaystyle W_{1,\perp}:=(z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}})-W_{1,//}.

Clearly, we have
z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}}=W_{1,//}+W_{1,\perp}
,
W_{1,//}\mathrel{\!/\mkern-5.0mu/\!}Z_{2}\mathrel{\!/\mkern-5.0mu/\!}x-y
, and
W_{1,\perp}\perp Z_{2}
.

Finally, by taking
\epsilon^{\prime}(q_{1}):=\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{2}|z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}}|}{6|x-y|},1-q_{1}\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle z^{\prime\prime}-q_{2}x-(1-q_{2})y\in\overline{xy},

and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<|x-y|(q_{2}-q_{1})<|z^{\prime\prime}-q_{1}x-(1-q_{1})y|\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}

\displaystyle<|z^{\prime\prime}-z_{q_{1}}|Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2}

\displaystyle=|W_{1,//}|,

for any
q_{1}<q_{2}<q_{1}+\epsilon^{\prime}(q_{1})
.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle|W_{1,//}-|x-y|(q_{2}-q_{1})Z_{2}|<|W_{1,//}|,

and we deduce that

\displaystyle d\bigg(q_{2}x+(1-q_{2})y,\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)}\bigg)<d\left(q_{1}x+(1-q_{1})y,\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{i},d)}\right)

for any
q_{1}<q_{2}<q_{1}+\epsilon^{\prime}(q_{1})
.
∎

Now, we introduce one more property regarding the geometry of
\Omega
:

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose that
\Omega
satisfies
Assumption A
with
\delta
,
d
as given in
Lemma
2.1
. Then,
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
is pathwise connected; that is, given
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, there exists a continuous curve in
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
joining
x^{\prime}_{1}
and
x^{\prime}_{2}
.

Proof.

Given
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
. Since the domain
\Omega
is connected,
\Omega
is pathwise connected. Hence, there exist
l\in\mathbb{N},\{\widetilde{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}\in\Omega
such that the following curve

\displaystyle\phi(s)=\begin{cases}[1-(l+1)s]x^{\prime}_{1}+(l+1)s\widetilde{x}_{1},\ &\mathrm{for}\ 0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{l+1},\\
[1-(l+1)s+j]\widetilde{x}_{j}+[(l+1)s-j]\widetilde{x}_{j+1},\ &\mathrm{for}\ \frac{j}{l+1}\leq s\leq\frac{j+1}{l+1},\,1\leq j\leq l-1,\\
[1-(l+1)s+l]\widetilde{x}_{l}+[(l+1)s-l]x^{\prime}_{2},\ &\mathrm{for}\ \frac{l}{l+1}\leq s\leq 1,\end{cases}

lies within
\Omega
.

As
Remark
2.2
shows, we have

\{\phi(s)\mid 0\leq s\leq 1\}\cap\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{m_{1}}B(x^{0}_{i},\frac{d}{4}).

Without loss of generality, we can assume that there is a function
k:\mathbb{N}\cap[1,l]\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}\cap[1,m_{1}]
such that
k(l-1)=k(l)
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{x}_{1}&\in\partial B(x^{0}_{k(1)},\frac{d}{4}),\\
\overline{\widetilde{x}_{j}\widetilde{x}_{j+1}}&\subset\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},\frac{d}{4})},\,|\widetilde{x}_{j}-\widetilde{x}_{j+1}|\leq\frac{d}{10},\quad\forall\,j=1,...,l-1,\\
\widetilde{x}_{l}&\in\partial B(x^{0}_{k(l)},\frac{d}{4}),\end{aligned}\right.

and that

\displaystyle\overline{x^{\prime}_{1}\widetilde{x}_{1}}\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}},\ \overline{\widetilde{x}_{l}x^{\prime}_{2}}\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}.

Indeed, given
0\leq j\leq l
a segment
\overline{\widetilde{x}_{j}\widetilde{x}_{j+1}}
(here we consider
\widetilde{x}_{0}:=x^{\prime}_{1}
,
\widetilde{x}_{l+1}:=x^{\prime}_{2}
), and a number
1\leq k\leq m_{1}
, the set
\overline{\widetilde{x}_{j}\widetilde{x}_{j+1}}\cap\partial B(x_{k}^{0},\frac{d}{4})
is finite. In fact, after adding finite subsections of the line, we can assume that either the entire line segment
\overline{\widetilde{x}_{j}\widetilde{x}_{j+1}}
lies within a ball
\overline{B(x_{k(j)}^{0},\frac{d}{4})}
for some
1\leq k(j)\leq m_{1}
or belongs to
\cap_{k=1}^{m_{1}}B^{c}(x_{k}^{0},\frac{d}{4})\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
.

Figure 2.2.
Example of construction of the re-indexation of k

Now, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(s)=\begin{cases}[1-2(l+1)s]x^{\prime}_{1}+2(l+1)s\widetilde{x}_{1},\ &\mathrm{for}\ 0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2(l+1)},\\
[2-2(l+1)s]\widetilde{x}_{1}+[2(l+1)s-1]\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{1},\ &\mathrm{for}\ \frac{1}{2(l+1)}\leq s\leq\frac{1}{l+1},\\
[1-(l+1)s+j]\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+[(l+1)s-j]\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\ &\mathrm{for}\ \frac{j}{l+1}\leq s\leq\frac{j+1}{l+1},\ 1\leq j\leq l-1,\\
[1-2(l+1)s+2l]\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{l}+[2(l+1)s-2l]\widetilde{x}_{l},\ &\mathrm{for}\ \frac{l}{l+1}\leq s\leq\frac{2l+1}{2(l+1)},\\
[2-2(l+1)s+2l]\widetilde{x}_{l}+[2(l+1)s-2l-1]x^{\prime}_{2},\ &\mathrm{for}\ \frac{2l+1}{2(l+1)}\leq s\leq 1,\end{cases}

where

(2.12)

\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}:=\widetilde{x}_{j}-\frac{d}{2}n(x^{0}_{k(j)})\ ,\ 1\leq j\leq l.

Now, we show that this new curve belongs to
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
. To do this, it suffices to show that for
1\leq j\leq l-1
,

\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1}},\overline{\widetilde{x}_{1}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{1}},\overline{\widetilde{x}_{l}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{l}}\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}.

To show that
\overline{\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1}}\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
for
1\leq j\leq l-1
, we re-parametrize
\widetilde{x}_{j}
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{j}=x^{0}_{k(j)}+\widetilde{x}_{j,1}e^{1}_{k(j)}+\widetilde{x}_{j,2}e^{2}_{k(j)}-\widetilde{x}_{j,3}n(x^{0}_{k(j)}).

We deduce that

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}=x^{0}_{k(j)}+\widetilde{x}_{j,1}e^{1}_{k(j)}+\widetilde{x}_{j,2}e^{2}_{k(j)}-\left(\widetilde{x}_{j,3}+\frac{d}{2}\right)n(x^{0}_{k(j)}).

Here, we recall that
\{e^{1}_{k(j)},e^{2}_{k(j)},n(x^{0}_{k(j)})\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Notice that
\overline{\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1}}\subset\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{k(j)},d)
. Therefore, for
0\leq s\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\partial\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\partial(\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d)))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\min\{d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\overline{\Omega}\cap\partial B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d)),d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d)})\}.

Notice that

d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\overline{\Omega}\cap\partial B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d))\geq d(B(x^{0}_{k(j)},d),\partial B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d))=2d.

Since
|\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}-x^{0}_{k(j)}|\leq|\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}-\widetilde{x}_{j}|+|\widetilde{x}_{j}-x^{0}_{k(j)}|\leq\frac{3d}{4}
, we have
\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}\in B(x^{0}_{k(j)},\frac{3d}{2})
. By
Corollary
2.1
we have

(2.13)

d(\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d)})\geq\frac{d}{4}

We also notice that, using the same argument as (
2.11
), we have
|n(x^{0}_{k(j)})-n(x^{0}_{k(j+1)})|<\frac{1}{50}
for
1\leq j\leq l-1
, from which we deduce that

(2.14)

|\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}-\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1}|\leq|\widetilde{x}_{j}-\widetilde{x}_{j+1}|+\frac{d}{2}|n(x^{0}_{k(j)})-n(x^{0}_{k(j+1)})|\leq\frac{d}{10}+\frac{d}{100}<\frac{d}{9}.

Hence, for any
0\leq s\leq 1

\displaystyle d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},3d)})\geq\frac{d}{4}-\frac{sd}{9}\geq\frac{d}{8}.

Therefore, we conclude that for any
0\leq s\leq 1

\displaystyle d((1-s)\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}+s\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1},\partial\Omega)\geq\frac{d}{8}.

We conclude that

(2.15)

\overline{\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j+1}}\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}},

for
2\leq j\leq l-2
.

For the case
j=1
or
l
, we use
Lemma
2.3
. Notice that for
j=1
or
l
, we have
\widetilde{x}_{j}\in\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},\frac{d}{4})},\,\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}\in\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},\frac{3d}{4})}
. Since for
i=1
or
l

\sqrt{\widetilde{x}^{2}_{j,1}+\widetilde{x}^{2}_{j,2}}\leq\frac{d}{4},

and

\frac{\widetilde{x}_{j}-\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}}{|\widetilde{x}_{j}-\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}|}\cdot n(x^{0}_{k(j)})=1,

all the assumptions of
Lemma
2.3
are satisfied. Hence, we deduce that

d(\overline{\widetilde{x}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}},\partial\Omega)=d(\overline{\widetilde{x}_{j}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}_{j}},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},d)})=d(\widetilde{x}_{j},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{k(j)},d)})\geq\frac{d}{8},

for
j=1
or
l
. We conclude that the curve
\widetilde{\phi}(s)
is the desired curve between
x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}^{\prime}
which belongs to
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
.

∎

Before we proceed, for given
\delta
,
d
, we define
\mathbb{Y}:=\mathbb{Y}_{1}\cup\mathbb{Y}_{2}
, where

\mathbb{Y}_{1}:=\{y_{i}^{0}\mid 1\leq i\leq m_{1}\},\,\mathbb{Y}_{2}:=\{y_{i}^{0}\mid m_{1}+1\leq i\leq m_{1}+m_{2}\}.

Now, we introduce a useful property of
\mathbb{Y}
:

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose that
\Omega
satisfies
Assumption A
with
\delta
,
d
as given in
Lemma
2.1
. Given
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in\Omega
. Then, there exist
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})}\in\mathbb{Y}
such that
\{x^{\prime}_{1},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})},x^{\prime}_{2}\}
forms a zigzag in
\Omega
, that is,

\displaystyle\overline{x^{\prime}_{1}y_{1}}\in\Omega,\ \overline{y_{N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})}x^{\prime}_{2}}\in\Omega,\ \overline{y_{i}y_{i+1}}\in\Omega,\ \forall 1\leq i\leq N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})-1.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{1}y_{1}},\partial\Omega)\geq\min\left\{d(x^{\prime}_{1},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\right\},

\displaystyle d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{2}y_{N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})}},\partial\Omega)\geq\min\left\{d(x^{\prime}_{2},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\right\},

\displaystyle d(\overline{y_{i},y_{i+1}},\partial\Omega)>\frac{d}{10},

for
1\leq i\leq N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})-1
.
We call such a zigzag a “good zigzag” from
x^{\prime}_{1}
to
x^{\prime}_{2}
with number of segments
N(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})+1
.

Proof.

Given
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in\Omega
, we shall show that there exist
y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathbb{Y}
such that

(2.16)

d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{\prime}_{i}},\partial\Omega)\geq\min\left\{d(x^{\prime}_{i},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\right\},

for
i=1,2
. Indeed, if
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, then by
Lemma
2.1
, there exists
1\leq l(i)\leq m_{1}
such that
x^{\prime}_{i}\in B(x_{l(i)}^{0},\frac{d}{4})
and we can parameterize the boundary of
\Omega
near
x^{0}_{l(i)}
as follows:

\displaystyle\partial\Omega\cap B(x_{l(i)}^{0},d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{x_{l(i)}^{0}+u_{1}e_{l(i)}^{1}+u_{2}e_{l(i)}^{2}-\phi_{l(i)}(u_{1},u_{2})n(x^{0}_{l(i)})\mid

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046ptx_{l(i)}^{0}+u_{1}e_{l(i)}^{1}+u_{2}e_{l(i)}^{2}-\phi_{l(i)}(u_{1},u_{2})n(x^{0}_{l(i)})\in B(x_{l(i)}^{0},d)\}

with
|\nabla\phi_{l(i)}|<\frac{1}{100}
.
We shall show that

(2.17)

\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}}\subset B(x_{l(i)}^{0},d)\cap\Omega.

Observe that,
\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}}\subset B(x_{l(i)}^{0},d)
by the convexity of
B(x_{l(i)}^{0},d)
, so it suffices to show that
\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}}\subset\Omega
. To show this, we define
s^{\prime}:=\inf{\{s\mid sx^{\prime}_{i}+(1-s)y^{0}_{l(i)}\notin\Omega\}}
. If
\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}}\not\subset\Omega
, we have
0\leq s^{\prime}<1
. Notice that
0<s^{\prime}
since
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\Omega
and
\Omega
are open. Next, we observe that
z:=s^{\prime}x^{\prime}_{i}+(1-s^{\prime})y^{0}_{l(i)}\in\partial\Omega
. Now, we recall the
Lemma
2.1
. We can re-parametrize
y^{0}_{l(i)}
,
z
and
x^{\prime}_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle y^{0}_{l(i)}

\displaystyle=x^{0}_{l(i)}+0u_{1}+0u_{2}-\frac{3d}{4}n(x^{0}_{l(i)}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=x^{0}_{l(i)}+z_{1}u_{1}+z_{2}u_{2}-z_{3}n(x^{0}_{l(i)}),

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=x^{0}_{l(i)}+x^{\prime}_{i,1}u_{1}+x^{\prime}_{i,2}u_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,3}n(x^{0}_{l(i)}).

Since
x^{\prime}_{i}\in B(x_{l(i)}^{0},\frac{d}{4})
, we have

\displaystyle|x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}\leq|x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}+|x^{\prime}_{i,3}|^{2}=|x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{0}_{l(i)}|^{2}\leq\frac{d^{2}}{16}.

Now, we consider the following function
\psi:[0,\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\psi(\zeta):=\phi_{l(i)}(z_{1}+\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,1}-z_{1}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}\zeta,z_{2}+\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,2}-z_{2}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}\zeta).

By the mean value theorem, there exists
\zeta^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\zeta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\psi\left(\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}\right)-\psi(0)}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}-0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,3}-\phi_{l(i)}(z_{1},z_{2})}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}.

Here we used the fact that
x^{\prime}_{i,3}\geq\phi_{l(i)}(x^{\prime}_{i,1},x^{\prime}_{i,2})
from the fact that
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\Omega
and (
2.2
).

Note that
y^{0}_{l(i)}
,
z
and
x^{\prime}_{i}
belong to a straight line, so we have

\displaystyle\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,3}-\phi_{l(i)}(z_{1},z_{2})}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi_{l(i)}(z_{1},z_{2})-\frac{3d}{4}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{100}-\frac{3}{4}=-\frac{74}{100}.

Here we use the fact that
z\in\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d}).

Finally, we get a contradiction to
\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}}\not\subset\Omega
by computing:

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{l(i)}\left(z_{1}+\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,1}-z_{1}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}\zeta^{\prime},z_{2}+\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,2}-z_{2}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}\zeta^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 21.33955pt\cdot\left(\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,1}-z_{1}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}},\frac{x^{\prime}_{i,2}-z_{2}}{\sqrt{|z_{1}-x^{\prime}_{i,1}|^{2}+|z_{2}-x^{\prime}_{i,2}|^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\zeta^{\prime})\leq-\frac{74}{100},

which implies that
|\nabla\phi_{l(i)}|\geq\frac{74}{100}>\frac{1}{100}
.
Hence, we deduce that (
2.17
) holds.

Hence, by (
2.17
), we have

d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial\Omega)\geq d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial(\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d))).

Next, we show that

d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial(\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)))\geq\min\{d(x^{\prime}_{i},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\}.

Since
\partial(\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d))\subset[\partial B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)\cap\overline{\Omega}]\cup[\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)})]
, we have

\begin{split}&d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial(\Omega\cap B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)))\\
\geq&d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},[\partial B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)\cap\overline{\Omega}]\cup[\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)})])\\
=&\min\{d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)\cap\overline{\Omega}),d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)})\}.\end{split}

By the convexity of
B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)
, we have

\displaystyle d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)\cap\overline{\Omega})\geq\frac{d}{4}\geq\min\{d(x^{\prime}_{i},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\}.

Next, we notice that

(2.18)

\frac{x^{\prime}_{i}-y^{0}_{l(i)}}{|x^{\prime}_{i}-y^{0}_{l(i)}|}\cdot n(x^{0}_{l(i)})\geq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}.

Now, we use
Lemma
2.3
. To show that all the assumption of
Lemma
2.3
is satisfied, we notice that we have
x^{\prime}_{i}\in B(x^{0}_{l(i)},\frac{d}{4})
,
y^{0}_{l(i)}=x^{0}_{l(i)}-\frac{3d}{4}n(x^{0}_{l(i)})\in\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},\frac{3d}{4})}
, and (
2.18
).
As a result, we deduce that

(2.19)

\begin{split}d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y^{0}_{l(i)}},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)})=d(x^{\prime}_{i},\partial\Omega\cap\overline{B(x^{0}_{l(i)},d)})\geq\min\{d(x^{\prime}_{i},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\}.\end{split}

We showed that (
2.16
) holds when
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
.

In the other case, namely when
x^{\prime}_{i}\notin\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, by
Lemma
2.2
, there exists
y\in\mathbb{Y}_{2}
s.t.
x^{\prime}_{i}\in B(y,\frac{d}{16})
. By using the fact that
B\left(x^{\prime}_{i},\frac{d}{8}\right)\cup B\left(y,\frac{d}{8}\right)\subset\Omega
, and that

\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y}\subset B\left(x^{\prime}_{i},\frac{d}{8}\right)\cup B\left(y,\frac{d}{8}\right),

we have

\displaystyle d(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y},\partial\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq d\left(\overline{x^{\prime}_{i}y},\partial\left[B\left(x^{\prime}_{i},\frac{d}{8}\right)\cup B\left(y,\frac{d}{8}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{(\frac{d}{8})^{2}-(\frac{d}{32})^{2}}>\frac{d}{10}.

Next, we show that for any
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbb{Y}
, there exists a zigzag which satisfies the condition mentioned in
Lemma
2.5
.

We consider
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbb{Y}
and define

(2.20)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{O}(w_{1}):=\bigg\{y\in\mathbb{Y}\mid\mathrm{There\ exists\ a\ zigzag}\,\ \{w_{1},u_{1},...,u_{s},y\}\ni\,u_{i}\in\mathbb{Y}\,\forall\,i=1,...,s,\\
&d(\overline{w_{1}u_{1}},\partial\Omega)>\frac{d}{10},d(\overline{u_{i}u_{i+1}},\partial\Omega)>\frac{d}{10}\forall\,1\leq i\leq s-1,d(\overline{u_{s}y},\partial\Omega)\>\frac{d}{10}\bigg\}.\end{split}

If
B(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\frac{d}{16}):=\{\widetilde{x}\mid\exists\,\widetilde{y}\in\mathbb{O}(w_{1})\ \ni\ |\widetilde{x}-\widetilde{y}|<\frac{d}{16}\}
covers
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, then
\mathbb{O}(w_{1})
covers
\mathbb{Y}
. We notice that
\mathbb{Y}\subset\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
. For a given point
y\in\mathbb{Y}
, there exists a point
x\in\mathbb{O}(w_{1})\subset\mathbb{Y}
with
|x-y|<\frac{d}{16}
and a zigzag
\{w_{1},u_{1},...,u_{s},x\}
such that

\displaystyle d(\overline{w_{1}u_{1}},\partial\Omega)>\frac{d}{10},\ d(\overline{u_{i}u_{i+1}},\partial\Omega)>\frac{d}{10},\ \forall 1\leq i\leq s-1,\ d(\overline{u_{s}x},\partial\Omega)>\frac{d}{10}.

We notice that

d(\overline{xy},\partial\Omega)\geq d\left(\overline{xy},\partial\left[B\left(x,\frac{d}{8}\right)\cup B\left(y,\frac{d}{8}\right)\right]\right)\geq\sqrt{(\frac{d}{8})^{2}-(\frac{d}{32})^{2}}>\frac{d}{10}.

Thus, the zigzag
\{w_{1},u_{1},...,u_{s},x,y\}
satisfies the condition mentioned in
Lemma
2.5
, and

\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{O}(w_{1}).

On the other hand, suppose that
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}\nsubseteq B\left(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\frac{d}{16}\right)
. We consider a point
\overline{w}_{3}\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
such that
d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\overline{w}_{3})\geq d/16
. By
Lemma
2.4
, there exists a continuous function
\overline{\phi}:[0,1]\longrightarrow\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
such that

\overline{\phi}(0)=w_{1}
,
\overline{\phi}(1)=\overline{w}_{3}
.

Notice that
d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\overline{\phi}(\cdot))
is a continuous function from
[0,1]
to
\mathbb{R}
and that
d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\overline{\phi}(0))=0,\ d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\overline{\phi}(1))\geq\frac{d}{16}
. We define

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}:=\sup\left\{0\leq s\leq 1\mid d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\overline{\phi}(s))\leq\frac{d}{15}\right\}.

Now, we define
w_{3}:=\overline{\phi}(\overline{\xi})
. Notice that
\frac{d}{16}\leq d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),w_{3})\leq\frac{d}{15}
. Since
\mathbb{O}(w_{1})
is finite, there exists a point
z
in
\mathbb{O}(w_{1})
such that
\frac{d}{16}\leq d(z,w_{3})\leq\frac{d}{15}
.

By
Lemma
2.2
, since
w_{3}\in\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}
, there exists
w_{4}\in\mathbb{Y}_{2}
such that
d(w_{3},w_{4})<\frac{d}{16}
, which implies
d(z,w_{4})\leq d(w_{3},w_{4})+d(z,w_{3})<\frac{31}{240}d
. Hence, we deduce that

d(\overline{zw_{4}},\partial\Omega)\geq d\left(\overline{zw_{4}},\partial\left(B\left(z,\frac{d}{8}\right)\cup B\left(w_{4},\frac{d}{8}\right)\right)\right)\geq\sqrt{(\frac{d}{8})^{2}-(\frac{31d}{480})^{2}}>\frac{d}{10}.

Thus, we have that
w_{4}\in\mathbb{O}(w_{1})
. This implies that
\frac{d}{16}\leq d(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),w_{3})\leq d(w_{4},w_{3})<\frac{d}{16}
, which is a contradiction. We conclude that
\Omega-\Omega_{\frac{d}{8}}\subset B\left(\mathbb{O}(w_{1}),\frac{d}{16}\right)
. Therefore,
\mathbb{O}(w_{1})
contains
\mathbb{Y}
, and
w_{2}\in\mathbb{O}(w_{1})
, which shows that there exists a zigzag between
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
satisfying the conditions mentioned in
Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Next, we introduce a lemma which is similar to
Proposition 5.1
in
[
2
]
:

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose that
\Omega
satisfies
Assumption A
. Given
\delta
,
d
in
Lemma
2.1
,
v_{M}>0\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\epsilon>0
. Then there exists
t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})>0
such that given
0<\tau_{2}\leq t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})
, one can find
l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2})>0
such that for all
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
,
(x,v)\in B(x^{0}_{i},d)\times\overline{B(0,v_{M})}
,

(2.21)

\forall\,s\in[0,\tau_{2}],\,X_{s}(x,v)\in\Omega_{l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2})}\cap B(x_{i}^{0},d)

implies

(2.22)

\forall\,s\in[0,t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})],\,|V_{s}(x,v)-v|<\epsilon.

In fact, the numbers
t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})
and
l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2})
can be chosen as follows.

(2.23)

t_{\epsilon}(v_{M}):=\frac{\epsilon}{8\widetilde{C}(\Omega)v_{M}^{2}},\,l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2}):=\frac{\tau_{2}\epsilon}{64},

where
\widetilde{C}(\Omega)
is defined in
Lemma
2.1
.

Remark 2.3
.

Unlike
Proposition 5.1
,
[
2
]
, we do not need the lower bound of the velocity
v
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
0\leq\tau_{2}\leq t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})
. Assume that

(2.24)

\forall s\in[0,\tau_{2}],X_{s}(x,v)\in\Omega_{l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2})}\cap B(x_{i}^{0},d),

where
t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})
and
l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2})
are defined in (
2.23
).

For almost every
(x,v)\in B(x^{0}_{i},d)\times\overline{B(0,v_{M})}
, the trajectory
X_{s}(x,v)
admits finitely many rebounds at times

0<t_{1}<t_{2}<...<t_{r(x,v)}\leq t_{\epsilon}(v_{M}),

with corresponding reflection angles

\theta_{i}:=\frac{\pi}{2}-\arccos{|n(X_{t_{i}}(x,v))\cdot V_{t_{i}}(x,v)|},\,\forall 1\leq i\leq r(x,v).

If
r(x,v)=0
, then we observe that
V_{s}(x,v)=v
for all
s\in[0,t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})]
. Therefore, we assume that
r(x,v)\geq 1
.

We notice that the estimate

(2.25)

\forall s\in[0,t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})],\ |V_{s}(x,v)-v|<\epsilon

holds if

(2.26)

\sum_{i=1}^{r(x,v)}2\sin{\theta_{i}}<\frac{\epsilon}{|v|}.

Next, we show that for any
1\leq i\leq r(x,v)-1

(2.27)

|X_{t_{i}}(x,v)-X_{t_{i-1}}(x,v)|\geq\frac{\sin{\theta_{i}}}{\widetilde{C}(\Omega)}.

We invoke the
Remark
2.1
and use the uniform interior sphere condition to deduce that for any
0\leq s\leq\frac{\sin{\theta_{i}}}{\widetilde{C}(\Omega)}
, we have

(2.28)

X_{t_{i}}(x,v)-s\frac{v}{|v|}\in B\left(X_{t_{i}}(x,v),\frac{1}{2\widetilde{C}(\Omega)}\right)\subset\Omega,

which directly implies (
2.27
).

Therefore, it suffices to show that

(2.29)

\sum_{i=2}^{r(x,v)}|X_{t_{i}}(x,v)-X_{t_{i-1}}(x,v)|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{8|v|\widetilde{C}(\Omega)},

and that

(2.30)

\sin{\theta_{1}}<\frac{\epsilon}{4|v|}.

The (
2.29
) holds since we have

(2.31)

\sum_{i=2}^{r(x,v)}|X_{t_{i}}(x,v)-X_{t_{i-1}}(x,v)|\leq t_{\epsilon}(v_{M})|v|\leq\frac{\epsilon|v|}{8\widetilde{C}(\Omega)v_{M}^{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{8|v|\widetilde{C}(\Omega)}.

Here, we use the fact that
|v|\leq v_{M}
.
For the (
2.30
), we again use the uniform interior sphere condition to deduce that for any
y\in\partial\Omega
and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
n(y)\cdot w<0
,
\theta_{w}:=\frac{\pi}{2}-\arccos{|n(y)\cdot w|}
, we have

(2.32)

d\left(y+sw,\partial\Omega\right)\geq d\left(y+sw,\partial B\left(y-\frac{1}{2\widetilde{C}(\Omega)}n(y),\frac{1}{2\widetilde{C}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\geq\frac{s\sin{\theta_{w}}}{2}|w|,

for any
0\leq s\leq\frac{\sin{\theta_{w}}}{2\widetilde{C}(\Omega)|w|}
.

Now, suppose that
\sin{\theta_{1}}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{4|v|}
. We consider the following estimate

(2.33)

\tau_{2}\leq t_{\epsilon}(v_{M}):=\frac{\epsilon}{8\widetilde{C}(\Omega)v_{M}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2\widetilde{C}(\Omega)|v|}\times\frac{\epsilon}{4|v|}\leq\frac{\sin{\theta_{1}}}{2\widetilde{C}(\Omega)|v|}.

Notice that if
r(x,v)\geq 2
, then
t_{2}\geq\frac{\sin{\theta_{1}}}{\widetilde{C}(\Omega)|v|}>t_{\epsilon}
, which is a contradiction.

For the case
r(x,v)=1
, since
X_{t_{1}}(x,v)\in\partial\Omega
, we deduce from (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) , by setting
y:=X_{t_{1}}(x,v)
, that

(2.34)

d(X_{s}(x,v),\partial\Omega)\geq\frac{|s-t_{1}|\sin{\theta_{1}}}{8}|v|\geq\frac{|s-t_{1}|\epsilon}{32},

for any
0\leq s\leq\tau_{2}
.
Hence, we obtain

(2.35)

\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau_{2}}d(X_{s}(x,v),\partial\Omega)\geq\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau_{2}}\frac{|s-t_{1}|\epsilon}{32}\geq\frac{\frac{\tau_{2}}{2}\epsilon}{32}=l_{\epsilon}(\tau_{2}),

which contradicts (
2.24
). We conclude the proof of
Lemma
2.6
.

∎

Now, we introduce the following notation:

Definition 2.1
.

Given
x,y\in\Omega
and
d\in(0,1]
, we define

(2.36)

\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(x,y):=\min\{N\mid\,\textit{There exist a good zigzag with length N from x to y}\},

and

(2.37)

\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(\Omega):=\max\{\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(x,y)\mid x,y\in\Omega\}<+\infty.

The last inequality is due to the fact that
\mathbb{Y}
is finite.

3.
Operator estimates and spreading properties

We introduce a new constant
n_{b}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\cos{\theta})\,d\sigma
and quote a key lemma (
Corollary 2.2
,
[
11
]
), which is very useful throughout this article:

Lemma 3.1
.

Given a measurable function g on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, suppose that the collision operator satisfies
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
. Then, there exists
C_{g}^{L}>0
which depends only on
n_{b}
,
C_{\Phi}
,
\rho_{g}
, and
e_{g}
(and
l_{g,p}
, where
p>\frac{3}{3+\gamma}
, if
\gamma<0
) such that

(3.1)

|L[g](v)|\leq C_{g}^{L}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}.

Definition 3.1
.

We define
C_{L}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}C^{L}_{f(t,x,\cdot)}
.

We also quote a very useful lemma which describes the spreading property of the operator
Q
(
Lemma 2.4
,
[
11
]
):

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that the collision operator satisfies
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
. Then, there exists a constant
C>0
, which depends only on
\gamma,\ \nu
, and
\ b_{0}
, such that for any
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
0<r\leq R
,
\xi\in(0,1)
, we have

(3.2)

Q^{+}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},R)},\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},r)}\right]\geq Cl_{b}c_{\Phi}R^{3+\gamma}\xi^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\sqrt{r^{2}+R^{2}}(1-\xi))},

where
l_{b}:=\inf\limits_{\frac{1}{4}\pi\leq\theta\leq\frac{3}{4}\pi}b(\cos{\theta})
.

In particular, when
r=R=\delta
, we have

(3.3)

Q^{+}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\delta)},\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\delta)}\right]\geq Cl_{b}c_{\Phi}\delta^{3+\gamma}\xi^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{0},\delta\sqrt{2}(1-\xi))}.

For simplicity, we define
C_{Q}:=Cl_{b}c_{\Phi}

Now, we will adapt an important lemma introduced in
Lemma 3.3
in
[
1
]
:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfy
Assumption A
and let the collision kernel
B
satisfy
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
. Suppose that
f(t,x,v)
is a continuous mild solution to (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) with
M>0
and
0<E_{f}<\infty
.
Then, there exist
x_{I}\in\Omega
with
\Delta^{0}\in\left(0,\;\min\left\{\frac{d}{10},\,\frac{1}{2}d(x_{I},\partial\Omega)\right\}\right]
,
v_{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}>0
, which depend on
\Omega,M,E_{f}
and the modulus of continuity of
f_{0}
,
such that for all
t\in[0,\Delta^{0}]
,
x\in B(x_{I},\Delta^{0})\cap\Omega
, we have

(3.4)

\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\ f(t,x,v)\geq\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{I},\Delta^{0})}(v).

Remark 3.1
.

The assumption here is weaker than that in
Lemma 3.3
of
[
1
]
: we require

f(t,x,v)
to be continuous only on
\Gamma_{conti}
, while
Lemma 3.3
of
[
1
]
assumes continuity on the larger set

[0,T)\times(\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}-\Gamma_{0})
.

Proof.

The following proof is an adaptation of the proof of
Lemma 3.3
in
[
1
]
.

First, we construct a lower bound at a point in
\Omega
. Note that by translation, we can always assume that
0\in\Omega
.
Since
\Omega
is bounded, we have for any
t>0

(3.5)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\Omega}(|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})f(t,x,v)\,dx\,dv\leq M(\text{\rm diam}(\Omega))^{2}+|\Omega|E_{f}<\infty.

Define
R_{0}:=\max\left\{1,\sqrt{\frac{2(M(\text{\rm diam}(\Omega))^{2}+|\Omega|E_{f})}{M}}\right\}
. Notice that
\Omega\subset B(0,R_{0})
. Then, we have

(3.6)

\int_{B(0,R_{0})}\int_{B(0,R_{0})\cap\Omega}f_{0}(x,v)\,dx\,dv=\int_{B(0,R_{0})}\int_{\Omega}f_{0}(x,v)\,dx\,dv\geq\frac{M}{2}>0.

Otherwise, we would have

(3.7)

\begin{split}&\int\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}-(B(0,R_{0})\cap\Omega)\times B(0,R_{0})}(|x|^{2}+|v|^{2})f_{0}(x,v)\,dx\,dv\\
>&R_{0}^{2}\frac{M}{2}\geq M(\text{\rm diam}(\Omega))^{2}+|\Omega|E_{f},\end{split}

which contradicts (
3.5
).

Hence, we can take a point
(x_{I},v_{I})\in\Omega\times B(0,R_{0})
such that
f(0,x_{I},v_{I})>\frac{M}{3|B(0,R_{0})||\Omega|}
. By the uniform continuity of
f_{0}(x,v)
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{B(0,2R_{0})}
, there exist
0<\Delta_{I}<\min\left\{\frac{d(x_{I},\partial\Omega)}{2},R_{0}\right\}
such that

f_{0}(x,v)\geq\frac{M}{6|B(0,R_{0})||\Omega|}

for any
(x,v)\in B(x_{I},\Delta_{I})\times B(v_{I},\Delta_{I})
.

Next, we observe that for any
(t,x,v)\in\left[0,\frac{\Delta_{I}}{4R_{0}}\right]\times B\left(x_{I},\frac{\Delta_{I}}{2}\right)\times B(v_{I},\Delta_{I})
, we have

(x-tv,v)\in B(x_{I},\Delta_{I})\times B(v_{I},\Delta_{I})

.
As a result, we deduce from (
1.12
) and
Lemma
3.1
that

(3.8)

\begin{split}f(t,x,v)&\geq f_{0}(X_{0,t}(x,v),v)\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}L[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds\right)\\
&\geq f_{0}(x-tv,v)e^{-tC_{L}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\\
&\geq\frac{M}{6|B(0,R_{0})||\Omega|}e^{-\frac{\Delta_{I}C_{L}}{4R_{0}}\left\langle 2R_{0}\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\end{split}

for any
(t,x,v)\in\left[0,\frac{\Delta_{I}}{4R_{0}}\right]\times B\left(x_{I},\frac{\Delta_{I}}{2}\right)\times B(v_{I},\Delta_{I})
, where the constant
C_{L}
depends only on
n_{b}
,
C_{\Phi}
and
E_{f}
(and
L_{f,p}
if
\gamma<0
).

We conclude the proof by defining

\Delta^{0}:=\min\left\{\frac{\Delta_{I}}{4R_{0}},\frac{\Delta_{I}}{2},\frac{d}{10}\right\}.

∎

To demonstrate the fact that given an initial lower bound around a point
x\in\Omega
, multiple diluting lower bounds can be generated at the same point, we quote
Lemma 3.3
,
[
1
]
:

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
Assumption A
. Let kernel
B
satisfy
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
. We consider a continuous mild solution
f
of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
). Suppose that there exist
A>0
,
\Delta_{1},\ \Delta_{2}>0
,
(\tau,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in[0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
B(x^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\subset\Omega
and

(3.9)

f(t,x,v)\geq A,\ \forall(t,x,v)\in[\tau,\tau+\Delta_{1}]\times B(x^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\times B(v^{\prime},\Delta_{2}).

Then, we have for
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
t\in[\tau,\tau+\Delta_{1}]
,
x\in B(x^{\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{n}})
,

(3.10)

f(t,x,v)\geq\alpha_{n}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)\mathbf{1}_{B(v^{\prime},r_{n}(\Delta_{2}))}(v),\,\forall\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

where the numbers
\{r_{n}(\Delta_{2})\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\{t_{n}(t,\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|)\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\{\alpha_{n}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
are defined as below:

\displaystyle r_{0}(\Delta_{2}):=\Delta_{2},\ r_{n+1}(\Delta_{2}):=\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}r_{n}(\Delta_{2}),

\displaystyle t_{n}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|):=\max\left\{\tau,t-\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{n+1}(2r_{n}(\Delta_{2})+|v^{\prime}|)}\right\},

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}:=A,

(3.11)

\begin{split}&\alpha_{n+1}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)\\
:=&\frac{C_{Q}}{2}r_{n}^{3+\gamma}(\Delta_{2})\int_{t_{n}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|)}^{t}e^{-(t-s)C_{L}\left\langle 2r_{n}(\Delta_{2})+|v^{\prime}|\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\alpha_{n}(\tau,s,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)^{2}ds\\
=&\frac{C_{Q}}{2}r_{n}^{3+\gamma}(\Delta_{2})\int_{0}^{t-t_{n}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|)}e^{-uC_{L}\left\langle 2r_{n}(\Delta_{2})+|v^{\prime}|\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\alpha_{n}(\tau,t-u,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)^{2}du.\end{split}

Proof.

The following proof is also a summary of the proof of
Lemma 3.3
in
[
1
]
.
We prove the proposition by induction on
n
. The case
n=0
is exactly the assumption. Assume that
Proposition
3.2
holds for
n=k
. Given
t\in[\tau,\tau+\Delta_{1}]
,
x\in B(x^{\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k+1}})
,
v\in B(0,|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))
, we first notice that when
s\in\left[\max\left\{\tau,t-\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k+1}(|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))}\right\},t\right]
, we have

|x^{\prime}-X_{s,t}(x,v)|=|x^{\prime}-x+tv-sv|\leq\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k+1}}+|t-s|(|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))\leq\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k}},

which implies that
X_{s,t}(x,v)\in B(x^{\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k}})\subset\Omega
.

We consider the second term of the right hand side of (
1.12
) to obtain the following lower bound:

(3.12)

\begin{split}&f(t,x,v)\\
\geq&\int_{\tau}^{t}\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{t}L[f(s^{\prime},X_{s^{\prime},t}(x,v),\cdot)](v))\,ds^{\prime}\right)Q^{+}[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot),f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds.\end{split}

Furthermore, by
Lemma
3.1
, we have

(3.13)

|L[f(s^{\prime},X_{s^{\prime},t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)|\leq C_{L}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}\leq C_{L}\left\langle|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2})\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}},

where the constant
C_{L}
depends only on
n_{b}
,
C_{\Phi}
and
E_{f}
(and
L_{f,p}
if
\gamma<0
).

Hence, the following estimate:

(3.14)

\begin{split}&f(t,x,v)\\
\geq&\int_{\tau}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)C_{L}\left\langle|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2})\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}\right)Q^{+}[f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot),f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),\cdot)](v)\,ds.\end{split}

Since we have
|X_{s,t}(x,v)-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k}}
, we can use the induction hypothesis:

(3.15)

f(s,X_{s,t}(x,v),w)\geq\alpha_{k}(\tau,s,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))}(w),\ \forall w\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

to deduce that

(3.16)

\begin{split}&f(t,x,v)\\
\geq&\int_{\max\left\{\tau,t-\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k+1}(2r_{k}(\Delta_{2})+|v^{\prime}|)}\right\}}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)C_{L}\left\langle|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2})\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}\right)\\
&\alpha^{2}_{k}(\tau,s,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)Q^{+}[1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))}(\cdot),1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))}(\cdot)](v)\,ds.\end{split}

Then we use
Lemma
3.2
to ”spread” the lower bound:

For any
\xi\in(0,1)
and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

(3.17)

Q^{+}\left[1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))},1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))}\right](w)\geq C_{Q}(r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))^{3+\gamma}\xi^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2})\sqrt{2}(1-\xi))}(w).

Hence, for any
\xi\in(0,1)

(3.18)

\begin{split}&f(t,x,v)\\
\geq&\int_{\max\left\{\tau,t-\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{k+1}(2r_{k}(\Delta_{2})+|v^{\prime}|)}\right\}}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)C_{L}\left\langle|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2})\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}\right)\\
&\alpha^{2}_{k}(\tau,s,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)C_{Q}(r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))^{3+\gamma}\xi^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{B(v^{\prime},r_{k}(\Delta_{2})\sqrt{2}(1-\xi))}(v)\,ds.\end{split}

Set
\xi=\frac{1}{4}
and notice that
B(v^{\prime},r_{k+1}(\Delta_{2})))\subset B(0,|v^{\prime}|+2r_{k}(\Delta_{2}))
. We conclude thus the proof of
Proposition
3.2
.

∎

Remark 3.2
.

Notice that by the last line of (
3.11
) and the fact that for any
\tau<t<t^{\prime}<\infty

\displaystyle t-t_{n}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|)\leq t^{\prime}-t_{n}(\tau,t^{\prime},\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|).

Hence, we notice that by induction on
n
, we can show that when
\tau<t
,
\alpha_{n+1}(\tau,t,\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)
is strictly decreasing with respect to
|v^{\prime}|
and strictly increasing with respect to
t
.

Now, we show that we can generate lower bounds starting from one point
x^{\prime}\in\Omega
and reaching another point
y\in\Omega
, as long as
\overline{x^{\prime}y}\subset\Omega
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\Delta_{2}\leq 2
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfy
Assumption A
and let the collision kernel
B
satisfy
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
. We consider a continuous mild solution
f
of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
). Suppose that there exists
A>0
,
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\in(0,1]
,
(\tau,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in[0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
B(x^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\subset\Omega
such that

f(t,x,v)\geq A,\ \forall(t,x,v)\in[\tau,\tau+\Delta_{1}]\times B(x^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\times B(v^{\prime},\Delta_{2}).

Then, given
\tau^{\prime}\in(\tau,\tau+\Delta_{1})
,
y\in\Omega
with
\overline{x^{\prime}y}\subset\Omega
, we have that
B\left(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)\subset\Omega
. Moreover, for all
x\in\Omega
,
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

(3.19)

\displaystyle f(t,x,v)\geq\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)\times B\left(\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},y,x^{\prime}),\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)}(x,v)

for any
m\geq m^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|,d(\overline{x^{\prime}y},\partial\Omega))
and
\tau^{\prime}\leq t\leq\min\{\tau+\Delta_{1},\tau^{\prime}+\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\}
.

Here, the functions
m^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})
,
\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3})
,
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},y,x^{\prime})
, and
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2})
are defined as

\displaystyle m^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\log_{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}}\left(\frac{\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+\mathfrak{a}_{1}+1}{\Delta_{2}}\right)\right\rceil,\left\lceil\log_{2}{\frac{\Delta_{2}+1}{\mathfrak{y}_{2}}}\right\rceil\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3}):=\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\frac{\tau^{\prime}+\tau}{2},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3}\right)e^{-C_{L}\left\langle\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+1\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},y,x^{\prime}):=\frac{2(y-x^{\prime})}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau},

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2}):=\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}\left(\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+1\right)}=\frac{\Delta_{2}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)}{2^{m+1}(2d_{\Omega}+\tau^{\prime}-\tau)}.

Here,
C_{L}
is defined in
Definition
3.1
,
\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\frac{\tau^{\prime}+\tau}{2},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3}\right)
is as defined in
Proposition
3.2
, and we denote
d_{\Omega}:=\text{\rm diam}(\Omega)
as the diameter of
\Omega
.

Proof.

For
0\leq\tau<\tau^{\prime}<\tau+\Delta_{1}
, we define
v^{\prime\prime}:=\frac{2(y-x^{\prime})}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}
and take
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
with

m\geq\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\log_{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}}\left(\frac{\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+|v^{\prime}|+1}{\Delta_{2}}\right)\right\rceil,\left\lceil\log_{2}{\frac{\Delta_{2}+1}{d(\overline{x^{\prime}y},\partial\Omega)}}\right\rceil\right\},

then we see that

r_{m}(\Delta_{2}):=(\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4})^{m}\Delta_{2}\geq\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+|v^{\prime}|+1

and
\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m}}<d(\overline{x^{\prime}y},\partial\Omega)
. Here, the number
r_{m}(\Delta_{2})
is as defined in
Proposition
3.2
and we notice that
\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}>1
.
Observe that we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\leq\frac{1}{2}d(\overline{x^{\prime}y},\partial\Omega)<d(y,\partial\Omega),

so that
B\left(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)\subset\Omega
.

Now, we prove the lower bound (
3.19
).
As long as
x\in B\left(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)
and
v\in B\left(v^{\prime\prime},\min\left\{\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}(t-\tau)},1\right\}\right)
, for

\tau^{\prime}\leq t\leq\min\left\{\tau+\Delta_{1},\tau^{\prime}+\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}\left(\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+1\right)}\right\},

we have

\begin{split}&\left|x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v-x^{\prime}\right|\\
\leq&|x-y|+\left|\frac{t-\tau}{2}v-(y-x^{\prime})\right|<\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}+\left|\frac{t-\tau}{2}v-\frac{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}{2}v^{\prime\prime}\right|\\
\leq&\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}+\left|\frac{t-\tau}{2}v-\frac{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}{2}v\right|+\left|\frac{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}{2}v-\frac{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}{2}v^{\prime\prime}\right|\\
<&\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}+\left|\frac{t-\tau^{\prime}}{2}v\right|+\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+2}}\\
<&\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}+\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+2}}+\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+2}}\\
=&\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m}},\end{split}

by which we deduce that

x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v\in B\left(x^{\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m}}\right)
.
Notice that we have

r_{m}(\Delta_{2})\geq|v^{\prime}|+\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+1\geq|v^{\prime}|+|v^{\prime\prime}|+1>|v^{\prime}|+|v|,

so we have
v\in B(v^{\prime},r_{m}(\Delta_{2}))
.

Note that for any
0\leq s\leq 1
, we have

\begin{split}&|sx+(1-s)\left(x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v\right)-(sy+(1-s)x^{\prime})|\\
\leq&s|x-y|+(1-s)\left|x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v-x^{\prime}\right|\\
\leq&\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m}}<d(\overline{x^{\prime}y},\partial\Omega).\end{split}

Hence, the segment
\{x-\sigma v|\ 0\leq\sigma\leq\frac{t-\tau}{2}\}
also lies within
\Omega
. From this, we deduce by using the first term of the right hand side of (
1.12
),
Lemma
3.1
, and
Proposition
3.2
that

(3.20)

\begin{split}f(t,x,v)&\geq f\left(\frac{t+\tau}{2},x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v,v\right)e^{-\frac{t-\tau}{2}C_{L}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}},\\
&\geq\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\frac{t+\tau}{2},\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|\right)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(x^{\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m}}\right)\times B(v^{\prime},r_{m}(\Delta_{2}))}(x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v,v)e^{-\frac{t-\tau}{2}C_{L}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\\
&=\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\frac{t+\tau}{2},\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|\right)e^{-\frac{t-\tau}{2}C_{L}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}},\end{split}

where we use the fact that

x-\frac{t-\tau}{2}v\in B(x^{\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m}})
and
v\in B(v^{\prime},r_{m}(\Delta_{2}))
.
Hence, we have the following inequality for
\tau^{\prime}\leq t\leq\min\bigg\{\tau+\Delta_{1},\tau^{\prime}+\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}(\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+1)}\bigg\}
,
x\in\Omega
,
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

\displaystyle f(t,x,v)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\frac{t+\tau}{2},\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|\right)e^{-\frac{t-\tau}{2}C_{L}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\mathbf{1}_{B\left(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)\times B\left(v^{\prime\prime},\min\left\{\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}(t-\tau)},1\right\}\right)}(x,v)

(3.21)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\frac{\tau^{\prime}+\tau}{2},\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|\right)e^{-C_{L}\left\langle\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime}-\tau}+1\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}}\mathbf{1}_{B\left(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)\times B\left(v^{\prime\prime},\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)}(x,v).

Here, we use
Remark
3.2
in the last inequality above.
The line (
3.21
) corresponds to (
3.19
). Thus, we have shown the statement of
Proposition
3.3
.

∎

The following corollary can be derived by modifying the starting time
\tau^{\prime}
in
Proposition
3.3
:

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfy
Assumption A
and let the collision kernel
B
satisfy
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
. We consider a continuous mild solution
f
of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
). Suppose that there exists
A>0
,
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
,
(\tau,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in[0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
B(x^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\subset\Omega
such that

(3.22)

f(t,x,v)\geq A,\ \forall(t,x,v)\in[\tau,\tau+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{1}^{\prime}]\times B(x^{\prime},\Delta_{2})\times B(v^{\prime},\Delta_{2}).

Then, given
y\in\Omega
such that
\overline{x^{\prime}y}\subset\Omega
, we have
B(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}})\subset\Omega
. Furthermore, for any
x\in\Omega,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

\displaystyle f(t,x,v)\geq\mathbb{B}(m,\tau,\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},A,|v^{\prime}|)\mathbf{1}_{B(y,\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}})\times B\left(\mathbb{V}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m,y,x^{\prime}),\frac{\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+1}}\right)}(x,v)

for any
m\geq\mathbb{M}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},|v^{\prime}|,d(\overline{x^{\prime}y},\partial\Omega))
and
\mathbb{T}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m)\leq t\leq\min\{\tau+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\tau+\Delta_{1}+\mathbb{D}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},m)\}
.

Here, the functions
\mathbb{M}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})
,
\mathbb{B}(m,\tau,\tau^{\prime},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3})
,
\mathbb{D}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},m)
,
\mathbb{T}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m)
, and
\mathbb{V}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m,y,x^{\prime})
are defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\log_{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}}\left(\frac{\frac{4d_{\Omega}}{\Delta_{1}}+\mathfrak{y}_{1}+1}{\Delta_{2}}\right)\right\rceil,\left\lceil\log_{2}{\frac{\Delta_{2}+1}{\mathfrak{y}_{2}}}\right\rceil\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}(m,\tau,\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3}):=\alpha_{m}\left(\tau,\tau+\frac{\Delta_{1}}{4},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3}\right)e^{-C_{L}\left\langle\frac{4d_{\Omega}}{\Delta_{1}}+1\right\rangle^{\gamma^{+}}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},m):=\frac{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+2}(4d_{\Omega}+\Delta_{1})},

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m):=\max\left\{\tau+\frac{\Delta_{1}}{2},\Delta_{1}+\tau-\frac{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+2}(4d_{\Omega}+\Delta_{1})}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m,y,x^{\prime}):=\frac{2(y-x^{\prime})}{\mathbb{T}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m)-\tau}=\frac{2(y-x^{\prime})}{\max\left\{\frac{\Delta_{1}}{2},\Delta_{1}-\frac{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}}{2^{m+2}(4d_{\Omega}+\Delta_{1})}\right\}}.

Remark 3.3
.

By
Remark
3.2
, we notice that

(1)

\mathbb{B}(m,\tau,\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},A,\mathfrak{y}_{3})
is strictly decreasing in
\mathfrak{y}_{3}
.

(2)

\mathbb{M}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})
is increasing in
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
and decreasing in
\mathfrak{y}_{2}
.

(3)

\mathbb{T}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m)
is increasing in
m
.

(4)

|\mathbb{V}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\tau,m,y,x^{\prime})|
is decreasing in
m
with the upper bound
\frac{4d_{\Omega}}{\Delta_{1}}
.

Now, we will use the multiple lower bounds around the initial point
x_{I}
from
Proposition
3.1
to derive lower bounds around the points
\{y^{0}_{i}\}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfy
Assumption A
and let the collision kernel
B
satisfy
Assumption B
with
\nu<0
. Fix
d
with
0<d<\min\{1,\delta\}
as in
Lemma
2.1
and a collection of points
\{y_{i}^{0}\}_{i=1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\subset\Omega
defined in
Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
.
Let
x_{I}
be as defined in
Proposition
3.1
.

Suppose that, for fixed
\alpha\in[0,1]
, there exists a continuous mild solution to (
1.1
)–(
1.3
).
Then, for any
\tau^{\prime\prime}\in(0,\Delta^{0})
, with
\Delta^{0}\in(0,\min\{\frac{d}{10},\frac{1}{2}d(x_{I},\partial\Omega)\}]
as in
Proposition
3.1
, there exist positive numbers
\mathfrak{R}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
,
\mathfrak{B}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
, and
\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
, depending on
\tau^{\prime\prime}
,
\Omega
,
M
, and
E_{f}
(and
L_{f,p}
if
\gamma<0
), and a family of velocity vectors

\{v_{i}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\}_{i=1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\subset B(0,\mathfrak{R}(\tau^{\prime\prime}))

such that

\bigcup_{i=1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}B\left(y_{i}^{0},\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\right)\subset\Omega.

Moreover, for each
1\leq i\leq m_{1}+m_{2}
, we have

(3.23)

f(t,x,v)\geq\ \mathfrak{B}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\mathbf{1}_{B\left(y_{i}^{0},\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\right)\times B\left(v_{i}(\tau^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\right)}(x,v),

for all

\tau^{\prime\prime}-\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\leq t\leq\tau^{\prime\prime}+\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime}).

Here, we use the same radius
\mathfrak{d}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
for the space and velocity balls and for the time window.

Proof.

First, by
Proposition
3.1
, we have

(3.24)

\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\ f(t,x,v)\geq\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{B(v_{I},\Delta^{0})}(v),

for all
t\in[0,\Delta^{0}]
,
x\in B(x_{I},\Delta^{0})
. Here, the quantities
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
,
x_{I}
,
v_{I}
,
\Delta^{0}
are defined in
Proposition
3.1
. Note that
B(x_{I},\Delta^{0})\subset\Omega
.
Consider a good zigzag
\{x_{I},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(x_{I},y_{i}^{0})}=y_{i}^{0}\}
in
\Omega
(cf.
Lemma
2.5
).

Given
0<\tau^{\prime\prime}<\Delta^{0}
, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and a
j
-tuple

\mathfrak{M}_{j}:=(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2},...,\mathfrak{m}_{j})\in\mathbb{N}^{j},

we define
\mathbb{B}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},|v_{I}|)
,
\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})
,
\mathbb{D}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})
,
\mathbb{V}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})
,
\mathbb{U}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})
,

\mathfrak{n}_{j}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{1}(\mathfrak{M}_{1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}):=\mathbb{T}(\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},0,\mathfrak{m}_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{1}(\mathfrak{M}_{1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}):=\mathbb{D}(\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\mathfrak{m}_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{1}(\mathfrak{M}_{1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}):=\mathbb{V}(\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},0,\mathfrak{m}_{1},y_{1},x_{I}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{1}(\mathfrak{M}_{1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},|v_{I}|):=\mathbb{B}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},0,\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},|v_{I}|),

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{1}:=\Delta^{0}.

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{j+1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j+1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}):=\mathbb{T}\left(\tau^{\prime\prime}-\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{j}(\mathfrak{m}_{k}+1)}},\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\mathfrak{m}_{j+1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{j+1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j+1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})=\mathbb{D}\left(\tau^{\prime\prime}-\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{j}(\mathfrak{m}_{k}+1)}},\mathfrak{m}_{j+1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{j+1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j+1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}):=\mathbb{V}\left(\tau^{\prime\prime}-\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{j}(\mathfrak{m}_{k}+1)}},\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\mathfrak{m}_{j+1},y_{j+1},y_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89418pt=\frac{2(y_{j+1}-y_{j})}{\mathbb{T}_{j+1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j+1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})-\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})},

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{j+1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j+1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},|v_{I}|)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt:=\mathbb{B}\Bigg(\mathfrak{m}_{j+1},\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\tau^{\prime\prime}-\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{j}(\mathfrak{m}_{k}+1)}},

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59596pt\mathbb{B}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},|v_{I}|),\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})-\mathbb{T}_{j-1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j-1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{j+1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j+1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}):=\min\{\mathbb{U}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\tau^{\prime\prime}+\mathbb{D}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})\}.

Here, the notation
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{T}
,
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{V}
are defined in

Corollary
3.4
. We also define
\mathbb{T}_{0}:=0
by convention. By
Remark
3.4
, we have
\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})<\tau^{\prime\prime}
and that all denominators
\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{M}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})-\mathbb{T}_{j-1}(\mathfrak{M}_{j}-1,\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})
are positive.
We also define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{1}:=\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\log_{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}}\left(\frac{\frac{4d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime\prime}}+|v_{I}|+1}{\Delta^{0}}\right)\right\rceil,\left\lceil\log_{2}{\frac{\Delta^{0}+1}{\min\{d(x_{I},\partial\Omega),\frac{d}{10}\}}}\right\rceil\right\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{j}(m):=(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2},...,\mathfrak{n}_{j-1},m),\,\mathfrak{N}_{j}:=\mathfrak{N}_{j}(\mathfrak{n}_{j}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}:=\max\Bigg\{1,\left\lceil\log_{\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{4}}\left(\frac{\frac{4d_{\Omega}}{\tau^{\prime\prime}-\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})}+\frac{2d_{\Omega}}{\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})-\mathbb{T}_{j-1}(\mathfrak{N}_{j-1},\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})}+1}{\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{j}(\mathfrak{n}_{k}+1)}}}\right)\right\rceil,

\displaystyle\hskip 68.28644pt\left\lceil\log_{2}{\frac{\left(\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{j}(\mathfrak{n}_{k}+1)}}+1\right)}{\frac{d}{10}}}\right\rceil\Bigg\}.

Now, we claim that for
1\leq j\leq\operatorname{Conn}_{d}(x_{I},y_{i}^{0})
, we have

(3.25)

\begin{split}&f(t,x,v)\geq\ \mathbb{B}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j}(m),\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{0},|v_{I}|)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\mathbf{1}_{B\left(y_{j},\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{m+1+\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(\mathfrak{n}_{k}+1)}}\right)\times B\left(\mathbb{V}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j}(m),\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\frac{\Delta^{0}}{2^{m+1+\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(\mathfrak{n}_{k}+1)}}\right)}(x,v),\end{split}

for any

\begin{split}&\mathbb{T}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j}(m),\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})\leq t\\
\leq&\min\{\mathbb{U}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j}(m),\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0}),\tau^{\prime\prime}+\mathbb{D}_{j}(\mathfrak{N}_{j}(m),\tau^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{0})\}\end{split}

and
m\geq\mathfrak{n}_{j}
. Here,
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
is the constant defined in
Proposition
3.1
.

This will be proved by induction on
j
.

Step 1: base case: propagation of the lower bound from
x_{I}
to
y_{1}
.

For the case
j=1
, we consider the
Corollary
3.4
with
A=\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
,
\tau=0
,
\Delta_{1}=\tau^{\prime\prime}
,
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}=\Delta^{0}-\tau^{\prime\prime}
,
x^{\prime}=x_{I}
,
y=y_{1}
,
v^{\prime}=v_{I}
, and
\Delta_{2}=\Delta^{0}
. Notice that all the assumptions of
Corollary
3.4
are satisfied since

B(x_{I},\Delta^{0})\subset B(x_{I},\frac{1}{2}d(x_{I},\partial\Omega))\subset\Omega
and
\overline{x_{I}y_{1}}\in\Omega
and the lower bound (
3.22
